  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{MSE of the slow iterate}
We now turn to the MSE of the slow iterate. The argument follows the same
structure as for the fast iterate, with one additional difficulty: the update of
\(y_k\) is evaluated at \((x_k,y_k)\), whereas the contraction assumption is
available for the reduced dynamics evaluated at \((h(y_k),y_k)\). We therefore
need to control the tracking error of the fast iterate, which enters the slow
recursion as an additional residual term. 

For convenience, introduce

\[
    \Delta^g_{k} \triangleq \overline g(x_{k},y_{k})- \overline g(h(y_{k}),y_{k}),
    \qquad
    \phi_k \triangleq g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} )- \overline g(x_{k},y_{k}).
\]
Thus, \(\Delta^g_{k}\) measures the error due to replacing \(x_k\) by \(h(y_k)\) in
the noisy averaged dynamics, while \(\phi_k\) is the Markovian-noise fluctuations
around the averaged slow dynamics.

Using the update \(y_{k+1}=y_k-\betag(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} )\), we have
\[
    \hat y_{k+1}=\hat y_{k}-\betag(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ).
\]
Expanding the squared norm and adding/subtracting the reduced dynamics gives
\begin{align}
    \left \| \hat y_{k+1} \right\|^2
    =&
    \left \| \hat y_{k}-\betag(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ) \right\|^2
    \nonumber\\
    =&
    \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ) \right\rangle
    +\beta^2\left \| g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ) \right\|^2
    \nonumber\\
    =&
    \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \overline g(h(y_{k}),y_{k}) \right\rangle
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \overline g(x_{k},y_{k})-\overline g(h(y_{k}),y_{k}) \right\rangle
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    +\beta^2\left \| g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ) \right\|^2
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} )-\overline g(x_{k},y_{k}) \right\rangle
    \nonumber\\
    =&
    \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \overline g(h(y_{k}),y_{k}) \right\rangle
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \Delta^g_{k} \right\rangle
    \label{eq: norm error slow 1}
    \\
    &\quad
    +\beta^2\left \| g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ) \right\|^2
    \label{eq: norm error slow 2}
    \\
    &\quad
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \phi_k \right\rangle.
    \label{eq: norm error slow 3}
\end{align}

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        
                
                                                                                                                                                                       
                                                                                                                       
                                                                              
                                                                               
              
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        
                
                                                  
              
                                                                                  
                
                                                                                                                                                
              
                                                                                                                                       
               
                                                                                                                                                       
                                                                                                                       
                                                                                                         
             


By the strong monotonicity assumption A.\ref{assumption: Lip and Mono of g}, the
first two terms in \eqref{eq: norm error slow 1} yield a contraction. The
additional term involving \(\Delta^g_{k}\) measures the error induced by evaluating
the slow update at \(x_k\) instead of \(h(y_k)\). More precisely,
\begin{align*}
\eqref{eq: norm error slow 1}   &=\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    -2\beta \left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \overline g(h(y_{k}),y_{k}) \right\rangle
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \Delta^g_{k} \right\rangle
    \\
    &\leq
    \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    -2\beta \mu_g \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    +2\beta \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|\left \| \Delta^g_{k} \right\|
    \\
    &\leq
    \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    -2\beta \mu_g \left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    +\beta \mu_g\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    + {\beta \over \mu_g}\left \| \Delta^g_{k} \right\|^2
    \\
    &\leq
    (1-\beta \mu_g)\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    +{\beta \over \mu_g}\left \| \Delta^g_{k} \right\|^2
    \\
    &\leq
    (1-\beta \mu_g)\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    +\beta { L_g^2 \over \mu_g}\left \| \hat x_{k} \right\|^2 .
\end{align*}
In the first inequality, we used the strong monotonicity of the reduced slow
dynamics and Cauchy--Schwarz. In the second inequality, we used Young's
inequality \(2ab\leq \epsilon a^2+b^2/\epsilon\) with \(\epsilon=\mu_g\).
The last inequality follows from the Lipschitz property of \(\overline g(\cdot,\cdot)\):
\[
    \left \| \Delta^g_{k} \right\|
    =
    \left \| \overline g(x_{k},y_{k})-\overline g(h(y_{k}),y_{k}) \right\|
    \leq
    L_g \left \| x_k-h(y_k) \right\|
    =
    L_g\left \| \hat x_{k} \right\|
\]
We take $\beta < \mu_g^{-1}$ so that the factor \(1-\beta\mu_g\) is strictly contractive and
bounded away from zero. The remaining deterministic term in \eqref{eq: norm error slow 2} is bounded
using Lemma~\ref{lemma: bounds}: there exists \(\mathrm{M}_g>0\) such that
\[
    \beta^2\left \| g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} ) \right\|^2 \leq \beta^2\mathrm{M}_g^2 .
\]
Combining the previous bounds on
\eqref{eq: norm error slow 1}--\eqref{eq: norm error slow 3}, we obtain
\[
    \left \| \hat y_{k+1} \right\|^2
    \leq
    (1-\beta\mu_g)\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|^2
    + { L_g^2 \over \mu_g}\beta\left \| \hat x_{k} \right\|^2
    + \beta^2\mathrm{M}_g^2
    -2\beta\left \langle \hat y_{k}  , \phi_k \right\rangle.
\]
Iterating this recursion from time \(0\) to time \(k\), and then taking
expectations, gives
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| \hat y_{k+1} \right\|^2 \right ]
    \leq&
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k+1}\left \| \hat y_{0} \right\|^2
    + \mathrm{M}_g^2 \beta^2
    \sum_{j=0}^k (1-\beta \mu_g)^{j}
    \label{eq: mse slow 1}
    \\
    &+
    { L_g^2 \over \mu_g} \beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| \hat x_{j} \right\|^2 \right ]
    \label{eq: mse slow 2}
    \\
    &-
    2\beta
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    \left \langle \hat y_{j}  , \phi_j \right\rangle
     \right ].
    \label{eq: mse slow 3}
\end{align}




The first term in \eqref{eq: mse slow 1} is the contribution of the initial
condition and decays geometrically. The second term in \eqref{eq: mse slow 1}
is a geometric sum, and therefore
\begin{align*}
    \mathrm{M}_g^2 \beta^2
    \sum_{j=0}^k (1-\beta \mu_g)^j
    &=
    \mathrm{M}_g^2 \beta^2
    {1-(1-\beta \mu_g)^{k+1} \over \beta \mu_g}
    \\
    &\leq
    {\mathrm{M}_g^2 \over \mu_g}\beta .
\end{align*}

We now control the term \eqref{eq: mse slow 2}, which is the geometrically
weighted accumulation of the fast-iterate MSEs. 
     
    
           
                
                              
                         
                                         
                                               
    
                                                                                   
Using the upper bound we obtained for the fast iterate, we get
\begin{align*}
    { L_g^2 \over \mu_g} \beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| \hat x_{j} \right\|^2 \right ]
    \leq&
    { L_g^2 \over \mu_g} \beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    \Big(
        (1-\alpha\mu_f)^{j+1}\left \| \hat x_{0} \right\|^2
        + \mathrm{MSE}^{x}_\infty(\alpha, \beta)
    \Big)
    \\
    \leq&
    {L_g^2 \over \mu_g^2}\mathrm{MSE}^{x}_\infty(\alpha, \beta)
    \\& +
    { L_g^2 \over \mu_g} \beta (1-\alpha \mu_f)  
    \left \| \hat x_{0} \right\|^2\underbrace{\sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    (1-\alpha\mu_f)^{j}}_{S_k} \;.
    \\
        
                               
                                
                      
                    
                    
                  
            
                                          
                  
\end{align*}
For the term $S_k$, we simply remark that, for $j\in [k]$, it holds that 
\[(1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    (1-\alpha\mu_f)^{j} \leq (1-(\alpha \mu_f \wedge \beta \mu_g))^k. \]
Therefore, we can upper bound each term of the sum uniformly and obtain that 
\begin{align*}
    S_k \leq (k+1) (1-(\alpha \mu_f \wedge \beta \mu_g))^k \;. 
\end{align*}
Note that the bound is tight for $\alpha \mu_f = \beta \mu_g$. This leads to 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                              
                                                                              

                                                                  
                                                                             
                 
                
                  
            
                                          
               
        
            
            
                
                                              
                       
             
            
            
                                          
                  
        
           
            
            
                                          
                  
        
        
                                               
              
                                                                                        
\begin{align*}
    { L_g^2 \over \mu_g} \beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta \mu_g)^{k-j}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| \hat x_{j} \right\|^2 \right ]
    \leq&
    {L_g^2 \over \mu_g^2}\mathrm{MSE}^{x}_\infty(\alpha, \beta)
    + \beta \; {L_g^2 \over \mu_g}  
    \left \| \hat x_{0} \right\|^2 (k+1) (1- (\alpha \mu_f \wedge \beta \mu_g))^{k+1}  .
\end{align*}

                                                              
                
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     
              
It remains to control the Markovian-noise term \eqref{eq: mse slow 3}. As in
the fast-iterate analysis, we use the Poisson-equation decomposition from
Lemma~\ref{lemma : poisson mse}. In particular,
\begin{align*}
    -2\beta
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta\mu_g)^{k-j}
    \left \langle \hat y_{j}  , \phi_j \right\rangle
     \right ]
    \leq&
    2\beta
    \left | 
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (1-\beta\mu_g)^{k-j}
    \left \langle \hat y_{j}  , \phi_j \right\rangle
     \right ]\right | 
    \\
    \leq & 2 \beta \left (  {(L_{v_g} + \mathrm{M}_\xi L_{K} |\Xi| )\mathrm{M}_{\mathcal S} \mathrm{M}_g  \over \mu_g} +   \mathrm{M}_\xi \left ( {3 - \beta \mu_g \over 1 - \beta \mu_g} + {\mathrm{M}_g \over \mu_g} \right ) \right )  \\
    & + 2 \alpha {(L_{v_g} + \mathrm{M}_\xi L_{K} |\Xi| ) \mathrm{M}_{\mathcal S} \mathrm{M}_f\over \mu_g} 
\end{align*}
Combining this bound with the bounds on
\eqref{eq: mse slow 1} and \eqref{eq: mse slow 2}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| \hat y_{k+1} \right\|^2 \right ]
    \leq&
    (1-\beta\mu_g)^{k+1}\left \| \hat y_{0} \right\|^2
    + {\mathrm{M}_g^2\over \mu_g}\beta
    \\
    &+\beta \; {L_g^2 \over \mu_g}  
    \left \| \hat x_{0} \right\|^2 (k+1) (1- (\alpha \mu_f \wedge \beta \mu_g))^{k+1}
    \\
    &+
    {L_g^2\over \mu_g^2}\mathrm{MSE}^{x}_\infty(\alpha, \beta)
    +
    2\beta
    \left ( 
        {(L_{v_g} + \mathrm{M}_\xi L_{K} |\Xi| )\mathrm{M}_{\mathcal S} \mathrm{M}_g + \mathrm{M}_\xi \mathrm{M}_g \over \mu_g}
        + { 2 \mathrm{M}_\xi \over  1 - \beta \mu_g}
        + \mathrm{M}_\xi
    \right )  \\ 
    &+ 2 \alpha {(L_{v_g} + \mathrm{M}_\xi L_{K} |\Xi| ) \mathrm{M}_{\mathcal S} \mathrm{M}_f\over \mu_g}.
\end{align*}
               
                
                               
           
                         
                   
                        
           
                             
                             
                       
                                                      
           
       
        
        
                                
            
                   
                                
           
                                                               
                                               
                    
        
              
                                                                             
It remains to identify the constants, 
\begin{align}
    D_1 &\coloneqq \frac{L_g^2}{\mu_g}, \label{eq::D_1} \\
    D_2 &\coloneqq \frac{L_g^2}{\mu_g^2} \;,\label{eq::D_2} \\
    D_3 &\coloneqq 
        4 \mathrm{M}_\xi \;, \; \text{and} \label{eq::D_3} \\
    D_4 &\coloneqq \frac{\mathrm{M}_g^2}{\mu_g}  +   2
    \left ( 
        {(L_{v_g} + \mathrm{M}_\xi L_{K} |\Xi| )\mathrm{M}_{\mathcal S} \mathrm{M}_g + \mathrm{M}_\xi \mathrm{M}_g \over \mu_g}
        +  \mathrm{M}_\xi
    \right ) \;. \label{eq::D_4} \\
    D_5 &\coloneqq 2{(L_{v_g} + \mathrm{M}_\xi L_{K} |\Xi| ) \mathrm{M}_{\mathcal S} \mathrm{M}_f\over \mu_g}\;. \label{eq::D_5}
\end{align}
This proves the desired MSE bound for the slow iterate and concludes the proof
of the theorem.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm : lower bound}
    There exist a constant $C$ and a constant $\alpha_0>0$ and:
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item a TTSA satisfying all Assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm : mse} such that for all $\beta\le\alpha\le\alpha_0$:
            \begin{align*}
                \liminf_{k\to\infty} \mathbb{E} \left [ \|x_k-h(y_k)\|^2 \right ] \ge C\alpha\qquad \text{ and }\qquad 
                \liminf_{k\to\infty} \mathbb{E} \left [ \|y_{k}-y^*\|^2 \right ] \ge C\alpha;
            \end{align*}
        \item a TTSA satisfying all Assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm : bias} such that for all $\beta\le\alpha\le\alpha_0$:
            \begin{align*}
            \liminf_{k\to\infty} \|\mathbb{E} \left [ x_k \right ]-h(y^*)\| \ge C(\alpha + \beta)\qquad \text{ and }\qquad 
            \liminf_{k\to\infty} \|\mathbb{E} \left [ y_k \right ]-y^*\| \ge C(\alpha + \beta).
            \end{align*}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The proof of (i) and (ii) uses the same fast iterate $(x_k)$ that is defined as: 
    \begin{align*}
        x_{k+1} &= x_k - \alpha(f(x_k) + \xi_{k+1}),
    \end{align*}
    where $f(x) = {x \over{1+x}}$. The sequence of noises $(\xi_{k})$ is a sequence of zero-mean \emph{i.i.d.} scaled Rademacher random variables (\emph{i.e.}, such that $\mathbb{P}[\xi_k=1/2] = \mathbb{P}[\xi_k=-1/2]=0.5$). Note that the function $f$ is Lipschitz continuous with a Lipschitz-continunous derivative. We assume that $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and we set $x_0=0$. 
    
    We first show by induction on $k$ that the sequence $x_k$ is bounded almost surely, and more precisely that $x_k\in[-1/2, 1]$. Assume that this holds for some $k$ and recall that
    \begin{align*}
        x_{k+1} = x_k - \alpha\left(\frac{x_k}{1+x_k} + \xi_{k+1}\right).
    \end{align*}
    We distinguish two cases:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item If $\frac{x_k}{1+x_k} + \xi_k\ge0$, then 
        \begin{align*}
            x_{k+1} &\le x_k \le 1\\
            x_{k+1} &\ge x_k - \left(\frac{x_k}{1+x_k} + \xi_k\right) = \frac{x_k^2}{1+x_k} - \frac12 \ge -\frac12,
        \end{align*}
        where we used that $\alpha\le 1$ in the second line and the fact that $x^2/(1+x)\ge0$.
        \item If  $\frac{x_k}{1+x_k} + \xi_k\le0$, then 
        \begin{align*}
            x_{k+1} &\ge x_k\ge -\frac12\\
            x_{k+1} &\le x_k - \left(\frac{x_k}{1+x_k} + \xi_k\right) = \frac{x_k^2}{1+x_k} + \frac12 \le 1,
        \end{align*}
    \end{enumerate}

    As the behavior of $x_k$ does not depend on the slow iterates $(y_k)_k$, the sequence $(x_k)$ can be analyzed with standard tools from one-time-scale stochastic approximation. Here, we have $x^*=h(y_k)=0$. In particular, since near 0 this function has a second order approximation of $x-x^2$,from \cite{allmeier2024computingbiasconstantstepstochastic}, we know that the bias and the MSE of $x_k$ are of order $\alpha$ and asymptotically equal to:
    \begin{align*}
        \lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E} \left [ x_k \right ] = \lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E} \left [ (x_k)^2 \right ] = \frac\alpha{2c} + O(\alpha^2).
    \end{align*}
    for some positive constant $c$. Moreover, from \cite[Chapter~10]{Kushner1997StochasticAA}, when $\alpha$ is small and $k$ is large, the variable $x_k$ is close to a Gaussian distribution of variance $\alpha/2c$, which implies 
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:apx proof lower bound 2}
        \lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E} \left [ |x_k| \right ] = \sqrt{\alpha / d \pi}  + O(\alpha)
    \end{align}

    for some positive constant $d$. For the variable $y_k$, we consider two different cases to prove either (i) or (ii):
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item To prove \emph{(i)}, we set
        \begin{align*}
            y_{k+1} = y_k - \beta ( y_k - |x_k|).
        \end{align*}
        Unrolling the recurrence equation $y_{k+1} = y_k(1-\beta)+\beta|x_k|$ shows that
        \begin{align*}
            y_{k+1} &= \beta \sum_{i=0}^{k} (1-\beta)^{k-i}|x_i|.
        \end{align*}
        Combined with \eqref{eq:apx proof lower bound 2}, this shows that
        \begin{align*}
            \lim_{k\to\infty} \mathbb{E} \left [ y_{k} \right ] &= \beta \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} (1-\beta)^{i} \lim_{k\to\infty} \mathbb{E} \left [ |x_k| \right ] \\
            &= \lim_{k\to\infty} \mathbb{E} \left [ |x_k| \right ]\\
            &= \sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d \pi}} + O(\alpha).
        \end{align*}
        By using Jensen's inequality, this shows that: 
        \begin{align*}
            \lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E} \left [ (y_k)^2 \right ] &\ge \lim_{k\to\infty}(\mathbb{E} \left [ y_k \right ])^2\\
            &=\frac{\alpha}{d \pi} + O(\alpha^{3/2}).
        \end{align*}
        \item To prove \emph{(ii)}, we define $y_k$ as:
        \begin{align*}
            y_{k+1} = y_k - \beta ( y_k - x_k)
        \end{align*}
        This shows that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E} \left [ y_k \right ] = \lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E} \left [ x_k \right ] = \alpha/2c + O(\alpha^2) $.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We start by detailing the general outline of the proof. We begin by concatenating the TTSA updates to jointly analyze the bias of the fast and slow variables. Exploiting the differentiability of the underlying dynamics (Assumption B.~\ref{assump : derivability}), we apply a first-order Taylor expansion to linearize the updates, which yields a highly coupled linear dynamical system. To mathematically decouple these dynamics, we introduce a two-stage coordinate transformation. First, we block-triangularize the system by solving an algebraic Riccati equation. Then, we fully diagonalize the system by solving a Sylvester equation.

This transformation projects the bias into a new coordinate space governed by a diagonalized recursion. While this recursion features strong contracting terms, it is corrupted by higher-order perturbation residuals arising from the diagonalization process and the multiplication by the transformation matrices. However, the order of these perturbations will not affect the nature of the contractions due to our assumptions on the step sizes. The diagonal structure then allows us to unroll the recursion and bound each transformed component independently. Finally, because our transformation matrices possess finite norms, and by a simple triangular inequality we get the final bounds on the bias.

\textbf{Concatenating the iterates.} We start by deriving a recursion for the joint bias vector. 
In this proof, we work with deviations from the equilibrium and write
\[
    \tilde x_{k} \triangleq x_k-h(y^*),
    \qquad
    \text{ and we recall that }
    \hat y_{k} = y_k-y^*.
\]
We also introduce the centered Markovian-noise terms
\[
    \psi_k \triangleq f(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} )-\overline f(x_{k},y_{k}),
    \qquad
    \varphi_k \triangleq g(x_{k},y_{k},\xi_{k +1} )-\overline g(x_{k},y_{k}).
\]
For the fast iterate, we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k+1} \right ]
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]
       -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ \overline f(x_{k},y_{k}) \right ]
       -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_k \right ]
    \\
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]
       -\alpha
       \mathbb{E} \left [ 
       \overline{f}\bigl(h(y^*)+\tilde x_{k},\,y^*+\hat y_{k}\bigr) \right ]
       -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_k \right ]
    \\
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]
       -\alpha
       \mathbb{E} \left [ 
       \overline{f}(h(y^*),y^*)
       + J_{xx}\tilde x_{k}
       + J_{xy}\hat y_{k}
       + R_f(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right ]
       -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_k \right ]
    \\
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]
       -\alpha J_{xx}\mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]
       -\alpha J_{xy}\mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
       -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ R_f(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right ]
       -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_k \right ],
\end{align*}
where we used $\overline{f}(h(y^*),y^*)=0$ in the last line.

Similarly, for the slow iterate,
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k+1} \right ]
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
       -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ \overline g(x_{k},y_{k}) \right ]
       -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_k \right ]
    \\
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
       -\beta
       \mathbb{E} \left [ 
       \overline{g}\bigl(h(y^*)+\tilde x_{k},\,y^*+\hat y_{k}\bigr) \right ]
       -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_k \right ]
    \\
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
       -\beta
       \mathbb{E} \left [ 
       \overline{g}(h(y^*),y^*)
       + J_{yx}\tilde x_{k}
       + J_{yy}\hat y_{k}
       + R_g(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right ]
       -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_k \right ]
    \\
    &= \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
       -\beta J_{yx}\mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]
       -\beta J_{yy}\mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
       -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ R_g(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right ]
       -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_k \right ],
\end{align*}
where we used $\overline{g}(h(y^*),y^*)=0$.

In both expansions, we applied a first-order Taylor expansion of the averaged
dynamics around the equilibrium $(h(y^*),y^*)$. Since the second derivatives
of $\bar f$ and $\bar g$ are bounded by Assumption~B.\ref{assump : derivability},
there exists constant $L_R>0$ such that, for all $k\geq 0$,
\[
    \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right\|
    \leq
    L_R\bigl(\left \| \tilde x_{k} \right\|+\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|\bigr)^2,
    \qquad
    \left \| R_g(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right\|
    \leq
    L_R\bigl(\left \| \tilde x_{k} \right\|+\left \| \hat y_{k} \right\|\bigr)^2 .
\]


                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
                
                                                                                                      
                                                                                                                       
                                                                                                                                                                   
                                                                                                                                                                     
               
                                                                                               
                
                                                                                                   
                                                                                                                        
                                                                                                                                                                   
                                                                                                                                                                      
              

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
                                                                    

Concatenating the two bias vectors yields the joint recursion
\begin{align}
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
         \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k+1} \right ]  \\
         \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k+1} \right ] 
    \end{array} \right ]
    =
    \underbrace{\left [ \begin{array}{cc}
        I-\alpha J_{xx} &  -\alpha J_{xy} \\
        -\beta J_{yx} & I-\beta J_{yy}
    \end{array} \right ]}_{H_{\alpha,\beta}}
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
         \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]  \\
         \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ] 
    \end{array} \right ]
    +
    \underbrace{
    \begin{bmatrix}
          -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ R_f(\tilde x_{k}, \hat y_{k}) \right ]
          -\alpha \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_k \right ]
          \\
          -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ R_g(\tilde x_{k}, \hat y_{k}) \right ]
          -\beta \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_k \right ]
    \end{bmatrix}
    }_{\mathcal R_k}.
    \label{eq:concatenated-bias-recursion}
\end{align}
The matrix $H_{\alpha,\beta}$ is the linearized discrete-time dynamics of the
joint bias. Its block structure shows that the two components are still coupled:
the fast bias depends on the slow bias through the block $J_{xy}$, while the
slow bias depends on the fast bias through the block $J_{yx}$. This coupling is
the main reason why one cannot simply analyze the two bias coordinates
separately.

The next step is therefore to find a change of coordinates adapted to this
two-time-scale linear system. More precisely, following ideas from singular
perturbation theory \cite[Chapter~2]{kokotovic1999singular}, we construct
invertible transformations that turn $H_{\alpha,\beta}$ into a block-diagonal
matrix. In the transformed coordinates, the fast and slow bias components can
then be controlled separately, with their respective contraction rates. In the following, we drop the subscript $\alpha, \beta$ from the matrix $H$ for conciseness of notation.

                                                                                  
               
                                                                                                   
                                                                                   
                                                                                  
                  
                                                     
             
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                

\textbf{Triangularization through a Riccati equation.}
The first step is to remove the upper-right coupling in the linearized dynamics.
We look for an invertible change of coordinates $T$ such that
\[
    \Pi \triangleq T H T^{-1}
\]
is block lower triangular, or equivalently $H=T^{-1}\Pi T$.

We choose $T$ to be upper triangular with identity blocks on the diagonal:
\begin{align}
    T
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ],
    \qquad
    T^{-1}
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & -U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ],
    \label{eq:riccati-transformation}
\end{align}
where $U\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times d_y}$ is to be determined. To simplify the
notation, write the block decomposition of $H$ as
\[
    H
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         A & B \\
         C & D
    \end{array} \right ].
\]
Then a direct computation gives
\begin{align*}
    THT^{-1}
    &=
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ]
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         A & B \\
         C & D
    \end{array} \right ]
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & -U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ]
    \\
    &=
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         A+UC & R(U) \\
         C & D-CU
    \end{array} \right ],
\end{align*}
where
\[
    R(U)
    \triangleq
    -(A+UC)U+B+UD .
\]
Thus, the upper-right block is zero if and only if $U$ solves
\begin{align}
    R(U)
    =
    -(A+UC)U+B+UD
    =
    0.
    \label{eq:riccati-equation-U}
\end{align}
This is an algebraic Riccati equation for the unknown block $U$ \cite[Eq. 2.8]{kokotovic1999singular}. Any solution
of~\eqref{eq:riccati-equation-U} yields the block lower-triangular form
\[
    \Pi
    =
    THT^{-1}
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         A+UC & 0 \\
         C & D-CU
    \end{array} \right ].
\]
                              

Writing the Riccati equation in terms of the blocks of the TTSA dynamics gives
\begin{align}
     \frac{\beta}{\alpha} U J_{yx} U
     + J_{xx} U
     - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} U J_{yy}
     =
     J_{xy}.
     \label{eq:riccati-equation}
\end{align}
This equation is the singular-perturbation Riccati equation associated with the
linearized two-time-scale dynamics. Under Assumption~B.\ref{assump : derivability}, it is shown in \cite[Chapter~2, Eq.~2.13]{kokotovic1999singular} that when $\beta/\alpha$ small enough, this Riccati equation admits a solution of the form
\[
    U
    =
    \sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)^\ell \tilde{U}_\ell ,
\]
where the matrices $(\tilde{U}_\ell)_{\ell\geq 0}$ are independent of $\alpha$ and $\beta$. The matrix $\tilde{U}_0$ can be found by setting $\beta/\alpha=0$ in~\eqref{eq:riccati-equation} which gives
\[
    J_{xx}\tilde{U}_0=J_{xy}.
\]
Since $-J_{xx}$ is Hurwitz by Assumption~B.\ref{assump : derivability}, the matrix
$J_{xx}$ is invertible and $\tilde{U}_0=J_{xx}^{-1}J_{xy}$.
This shows that
\begin{align*}
    U
    &= \tilde{U}_0 
    +\sum_{\ell=1}^\infty \left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)^\ell \tilde{U}_\ell \\
    &=J_{xx}^{-1}J_{xy} + \frac{\beta}{\alpha}\hat{U}_{\alpha,\beta},
\end{align*}
where $\hat{U}_{\alpha,\beta}\triangleq\sum_{\ell=1}^\infty \left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{\ell-1} \tilde{U}_\ell$. 

From Assumption B.\ref{assump : derivability} and since we are assuming that ${\beta \over \alpha} < \min\{\varepsilon_{0}^*, \varepsilon_{1}^* \}$ where $\varepsilon_{0}^*, \varepsilon_{1}^*$ are defined in \eqref{eq: Riccati eps for the ball}, \eqref{eq: Riccati eps for the contraction} and are dependent on the predefined radius $R_{ric}= {1\over 4 \left \| G \right\|_{\text{op}} \sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm G)} M_J }$ we can apply Lemma~\ref{lemma: Uniform Bound Riccati} to show that the Riccati equation accepts a unique solution that lives inside the closed ball $B_{R_{ric}}^{U_0} = \{U : \|U - \tilde U_0\| \leq R_{ric}\}$. This implies that for the selected range of the ratio $\beta \over \alpha $ we have : 
\[
\left \| U \right\|_{\text{op}} \leq R_{ric} + \left \| U_0 \right\|_{\text{op}}
\]

We now apply the transformation to the linear recursion. Recall that
\[
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
         b^x_{k}  \\
         b^y_{k}
    \end{array} \right ]
    =
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \tilde x_{k} \right ]\\
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \hat y_{k} \right ]
    \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Using $H=T^{-1}\Pi T$, the recursion~\eqref{eq:concatenated-bias-recursion} becomes
\begin{align*}
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
         b^x_{k+1}  \\
         b^y_{k+1}
    \end{array} \right ]
    &=
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & -U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ]
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         
        I-\alpha J_{xx}-\beta UJ_{yx}
     & 0 \\
         
        -\beta J_{yx}
     & 
        I-\beta(J_{yy}-J_{yx}U)
    
    \end{array} \right ]
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ]
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
         b^x_{k}  \\
         b^y_{k}
    \end{array} \right ] +\mathcal R_k
    \\
             
       
                     
                                                            
                                   
              
                                                           
                                      
                    
\end{align*}
Define the transformed bias
\[
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        r^x_{k}  \\
        r^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ]
    \triangleq
    T\left [ \begin{array}{c}
         b^x_{k}  \\
         b^y_{k}
    \end{array} \right ]
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & U \\
         0 & I
    \end{array} \right ]\left [ \begin{array}{c}
         b^x_{k}  \\
         b^y_{k}
    \end{array} \right ].
\]
Multiplying the bias recursion by $T$ gives
\begin{align}
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        r^x_{k+1}  \\
        r^y_{k+1}
\end{array} \right ]
    &=
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         
        I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta}
     & 0 \\
         
        -\beta J_{yx}
     & 
        I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta}
    
    \end{array} \right ]
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        r^x_{k}  \\
        r^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ]
    +
    T \mathcal R_k,
    \label{eq:riccati-transformed-recursion}
\end{align}
where
\begin{align*}
    J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta}
    &\triangleq
    J_{xx}
    +
    \frac{\beta}{\alpha}U J_{yx}
    \\
    J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta}
    &\triangleq
    J_{yy}
    -
    J_{yx}J_{xx}^{-1}J_{xy}
    -
    \frac{\beta}{\alpha}J_{yx}\hat{U}_{\alpha,\beta}.
\end{align*}
In particular, the leading-order matrix for the slow variable's bias is
\[
    \Delta
    \triangleq
    J_{yy}
    -
    J_{yx}J_{xx}^{-1}J_{xy}.
\]
This matrix is the effective first-order drift of the slow dynamics. The assumption that $-\Delta$ is Hurwitz is standard
in two-time-scale stochastic approximation and appears, for instance, in the linear
and CLT analyses of TTSA
\cite{kwon2024twotimescalelinearstochasticapproximation, haque2025tightfinitetimebounds, hu2024centrallimittheoremtwotimescale, kaledin2020finite}.

For lighter notation, we write~\eqref{eq:riccati-transformed-recursion} as
\[
    r_{k+1}
    =
    \Pi r_k
    +
    T\mathcal R_k,
\]
where $r_k\triangleq \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        r^x_{k}  \\
        r^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ]$, and
 $   \Pi
    \triangleq
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         
        I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta}
     & 0 \\
         
        -\beta J_{yx}
     & 
        I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta}
    
    \end{array} \right ]$.
                              


                                                                                     

                                                             
               
                                                                                          
                                   
             
                                                                                     
                
                                              
              
                                                                                                                                                                                                                               
               
                                                                          
             
                                                                                                                      
                  
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          
                
                                                                                                                                                                                                               
                                                                               
                                                                              
                  
              
                                                                                                     
                
                                                                   
              
                                              
                
                                                                                                                                                                   
                                                                         
                                                                        
                  
              
                                                                                                                              
                                                                                                                                                                                       
                
                                                                                                                     
               
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        

                                                  
                
                                  
              

 
  \@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@} 
                {1.5ex \@plus 0.5ex \@minus 0.2ex} 
                {-1em} 
                { 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize\normalsize\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Block diagonalization through a Sylvester equation.}
After the Riccati transformation, the linear part of the recursion is block lower
triangular. The remaining coupling is the bottom left block $-\beta J_{yx}$, which still lets
the fast transformed bias influence the slow transformed bias. We now remove this
coupling by a second change of coordinates. Let
\begin{align}
    S
    \triangleq
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & 0 \\
         X & I
    \end{array} \right ],
    \qquad
    S^{-1}
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & 0 \\
         -X & I
    \end{array} \right ],
    \label{eq:sylvester-transformation}
\end{align}
where $X\in\mathbb R^{d_y\times d_x}$ is to be determined. A direct computation gives
\begin{align*}
    S\Pi S^{-1}
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta} & 0 \\
         X(I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta})-(I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta})X-\beta J_{yx} & I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta}
    \end{array} \right ].
\end{align*}
Hence, the bottom left block vanishes if and only if
\begin{align*}
    X(I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta})
    -
    (I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta})X
    -
    \beta J_{yx}
    =
    0.
\end{align*}
Equivalently, after simplifying and dividing by $\alpha$,
\begin{align}
    XJ_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta}
    -
    \frac{\beta}{\alpha}J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta} X
    =
    -\frac{\beta}{\alpha}J_{yx}.
    \label{eq:sylvester-equation-X}
\end{align}
This is a Sylvester equation for the unknown block $X$ \cite[Chapter~2, Eq~4.3]{kokotovic1999singular}, \cite[Lemma 8.3]{POZNYAK2008133}. From \cite[Lemma 8.3]{POZNYAK2008133}. 
Since we are assuming B.\ref{assump : derivability} and ${\beta \over \alpha } < \min\{ \varepsilon_{0}^*, \varepsilon_{1}^* , \varepsilon_{2}^*, \varepsilon_{3}^* \}$ that are dependent on $R_{ric}$ and are defined in \eqref{eq: Riccati eps for the ball}, \eqref{eq: Riccati eps for the contraction}, \eqref{eq: eps condition sylvester ball}, \eqref{eq: eps condition sylvester contraction} then the above Sylvester equation accepts a unique solution $X$ that lives inside a closed ball $B_{\varepsilon R_{syl}} = \{X : \left \| X \right\|_{\text{op}} \leq \varepsilon R_{syl}\}$ where $R_{syl} = 2 \left \| J_{yx} \right\|_{\text{op}} \left \|  J_{xx}^{-1} \right\|_{\text{op}}$, this directly implies that:
\begin{align}
    \left \| X \right\|_{\text{op}} \leq {\beta \over \alpha } R_{syl} 
    \label{eq:alpha-X-bound}
\end{align}

                                          
    
                              
            
                              
    
                                                       
    
                                     
    
                                                                              
                                             
                                                                                                                                                                        
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          
Furthermore, the solution admits the expansion 
\begin{align*}
    X  = \sum_{\ell = 1}^\infty \left ( \beta \over \alpha \right ) ^\ell \tilde X_\ell, 
\end{align*}
We can solve for $\tilde X _1$ by replacing the sum into \eqref{eq:sylvester-equation-X} then dividing by $\beta \over \alpha$ then setting it to zero to get:
\begin{align*}
    \tilde X _1 = -J_{yx}J_{xx}^{-1}
\end{align*}
With this choice of $X$, the transformed matrix $ \Lambda
    \triangleq
    S\Pi S^{-1}$ is block diagonal:
\[
    \Lambda
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta} & 0 \\
         0 & I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta}
    \end{array} \right ].
\]

                                                                 
                                                                           
                                                                             
                                                                                
                       
               
       
       
                               
            
            
       
                                
                                         
             
                                                                                
                                             
    
                            
                                        
                            
    
                      
    
         
       
                                     
    
                            
    
                 
       
                  
            
         
                         
             
    
                                                                       
               
                          
                                            
             
                                                   
                                               
               
                                                      
       
            
                                     
           
                                    
              
       
                                  
                                     
             
                                                                                                            
                                                                              
                                                                                
                                                                   
                                                           
               
       
       
                                            
       
                                               
                                             
             
                                                                                
                                                                                   
                                     
We now apply the second change of coordinates to the transformed bias vector
$r_k$. Define
\begin{align*}
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        s^x_{k}  \\
        s^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ]
    \triangleq
    S\left [ \begin{array}{c}
        r^x_{k}  \\
        r^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ]
    =
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & 0 \\
         X & I
    \end{array} \right ]\left [ \begin{array}{c}
        r^x_{k}  \\
        r^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ].
\end{align*}
By the choice of $X$, the linear part of the recursion is block diagonal. Hence,
using \[S T = \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & U \\
         X & XU+I
    \end{array} \right ],\] we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        s^x_{k+1}  \\
        s^y_{k+1}
\end{array} \right ]
    &= \Lambda 
    \left [ \begin{array}{c}
        s^x_{k}  \\
        s^y_{k}
\end{array} \right ]   -
    \left [ \begin{array}{cc}
         I & U \\
         X & XU+I
    \end{array} \right ]
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \alpha\bigl(
            \mathbb{E} \left [ R_f(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right ]
            +
            \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_k \right ]
        \bigr)
        \\
        \beta\bigl(
            \mathbb{E} \left [ R_g(\tilde x_{k},\hat y_{k}) \right ]
            +
            \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_k \right ]
        \bigr)
    \end{bmatrix}.
\end{align*}

Hence, the concatenated transformed bias $s_k$ satisfies the compact recursion
\begin{align}
    s_{k+1}
    =
    \Lambda s_k
    +
    ST\mathcal R_k.
    \label{eq:compact-transformed-bias-recursion}
\end{align}

                {1.5ex \@plus 0.5ex \@minus 0.2ex}

\@xpt\@xipt

  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Recursion on the transformed system.}
We now roll out the transformed recursion~\eqref{eq:compact-transformed-bias-recursion}. 
Since the matrix $\Lambda$ is block diagonal, we obtain
\begin{align*}
    s_{k+1}
    =
    \Lambda^{k+1}s_0
    +
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    \Lambda^{k-j}ST\mathcal R_j .
\end{align*}
This representation is the main payoff of the two transformations: the linear part
of the recursion is now decoupled into a fast block and a slow block. Writing the two
components separately gives
\begin{align}
    s^x_{k+1}
    =&\,
    (I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta})^{k+1}s^x_0
    -\alpha
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta})^{k-j}
    \left ( 
        \mathbb{E} \left [ R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right ]
        +
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_j \right ]
    \right ) 
    \nonumber\\
    &\,
    -\beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta})^{k-j}
    U
    \left ( 
        \mathbb{E} \left [ R_g(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right ]
        +
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_j \right ]
    \right ) ,
    \label{eq:sylvester-fast-bias}
    \\
    s^y_{k+1}
    =&\,
    (I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta})^{k+1}s^y_0
    -\alpha
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta})^{k-j}
    X
    \left ( 
        \mathbb{E} \left [ R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right ]
        +
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_j \right ]
    \right ) 
    \nonumber\\
    &\,
    -\beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    (I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta})^{k-j}
    (XU+I)
    \left ( 
        \mathbb{E} \left [ R_g(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right ]
        +
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_j \right ]
    \right )  .
    \label{eq:sylvester-slow-bias}
\end{align}

The original bias recursion was fully coupled: the fast and slow biases influenced each
other already at the level of the linear dynamics. After the changes of coordinates
defined by $T$ and $S$, this coupling has been pushed into the residual terms, while
the leading linear dynamics are block diagonal. This is precisely what allows us to
study the two components separately.

The next step is to show that the two diagonal blocks are contractions in suitable
Lyapunov norms. More precisely, for our choice on the step sizes, the matrices describing the perturbed dynamics:
\[
    I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha, \beta}
    \qquad\text{and}\qquad
    I-\beta J_{yy}^{\alpha, \beta}
\]
inherit the stability of $I- \alpha J_{xx}$ and $I - \beta \Delta$, respectively. Their powers therefore
produce the exponentially decaying initial-condition terms in the final bias bound,
with decay rates of order $\alpha$ for the fast component and $\beta$ for the slow
component.


 
  \@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@} 
                {1.5ex \@plus 0.5ex \@minus 0.2ex} 
                {-1em} 
                { 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize\normalsize\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Bound of the fast transformed component.}
We start from the recursion~\eqref{eq:sylvester-fast-bias}. For readability, write
\[
    A_x \triangleq I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha,\beta}.
\]
Applying the triangle inequality, submultiplicativity of the operator norm, and
Jensen's inequality gives
\begin{align*}
\left \| s^x_{k+1} \right\|
\leq\;&
    \left \| A_x^{k+1}s^x_0 \right\|
    +
    \alpha
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    \left \| A_x^{k-j} \right\|_{\text{op}}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
\\
&\quad
    +
    \beta
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    \left \| A_x^{k-j} \right\|_{\text{op}}
    \left \| U \right\|_{\text{op}}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_g(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
\\
&\quad
    +
    \alpha
    \left \| 
        \sum_{j=0}^k
        A_x^{k-j}
        \mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_j \right ]
     \right\|
    +
    \beta
    \left \| 
        \sum_{j=0}^k
        A_x^{k-j}
        U\mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_j \right ]
     \right\| .
\end{align*}
In the two terms involving $R_f$ and $R_g$, we used
$\left \| \mathbb{E} \left [ Z \right ] \right\|\leq \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| Z \right\| \right ]$. By contrast, we keep the Markovian-noise
terms as weighted sums, since these will be controlled later through the
Poisson equation argument.

The Euclidean operator norm does not directly reveal the contraction of
$A_x=I-\alpha J_{xx}^{\alpha,\beta}$. We therefore use a Lyapunov norm adapted to
the unperturbed fast linear dynamics. Since $-J_{xx}$ is Hurwitz by
Assumption~B.\ref{assump : derivability}, there exists a unique symmetric positive
definite matrix $\mathrm L$ solving
\begin{align}
    \mathrm L J_{xx} + J_{xx}^\top \mathrm L = I .
    \label{eq:lyapunov-equation-fast}
\end{align}
We denote the associated norm by
\[
    \left \| z \right\|_{\mathrm L}
    \triangleq
    \sqrt{z^\top \mathrm L z}.
\]
Since we are assuming B.\ref{assump : derivability}, $\alpha < \alpha^*(R_{ric})$ as defined in \eqref{eq: alpha condition fast Lyapunov} and the ratio satisfies ${\beta \over \alpha} < \min\{\varepsilon_{0}^*, \varepsilon_{1}^*,\varepsilon_{5}^*\}$ where $\varepsilon_{0}^*, \varepsilon_{1}^*,\varepsilon_{5}^*$ depend on $R_{ric}$ and are fully defined in \eqref{eq: Riccati eps for the ball}, \eqref{eq: Riccati eps for the contraction}, \eqref{eq: eps condition for fast lyapunov contraction} then after applying submultiplicativity of the norm we can apply Lemma~\ref{lemma: Lyapunov's fast contraction} to get:
we have:
\begin{align*}
    \left \| 
        (I-\alpha J_{xx}-\beta UJ_{yx})^{k}
     \right\|_{\mathrm L}\leq \left \| 
        I-\alpha J_{xx}-\beta UJ_{yx}
     \right\|_{\mathrm L}^k
    \leq
    e^{-kc\alpha}
\end{align*}
where $c = (8 \left \| \mathrm L \right\|_{\text{op}})^{-1}$, see Lemma~\ref{lemma: Lyapunov's fast contraction} and the proof for full derivation. Equivalently,
\[
    \left \| A_x^{k}
     \right\|_{\mathrm L}
    \leq
    e^{-kc\alpha}
\]
                                                                                                                                                
                
                                                               
              
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
               
                                                                                                                 
             
which yields
for every $\ell\geq 0$,
\[
    \left \| A_x^\ell \right\|_{\text{op}}
    \leq \sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} \left \| A_x^\ell \right\|_{\mathrm L}\leq 
    \sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} e^{-c\alpha \ell}.
\]
Applying this bound to~\eqref{eq:sylvester-fast-bias} gives
\begin{align}
\left \| s^x_{k+1} \right\|
\leq\;&
    \sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} e^{-(k+1)c\alpha}\left \| s^x_0 \right\|
    +
    \alpha\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)}
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    e^{-c\alpha(k-j)}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
    \label{eq:fast-bias-term-1}
\\
&\quad
    +
    \beta\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)}\left \| U \right\|_{\text{op}}
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    e^{-c\alpha(k-j)}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_g(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
    \label{eq:fast-bias-term-2}
\\
&\quad
    +
    \alpha
    \left \| 
        \sum_{j=0}^k
        A_x^{k-j}\mathbb{E} \left [ \psi_j \right ]
     \right\|
    +
    \beta
    \left \| 
        \sum_{j=0}^k
        A_x^{k-j}U\mathbb{E} \left [ \varphi_j \right ]
     \right\| .
    \label{eq:fast-bias-term-3}
\end{align}
We now bound the terms on the right hand side separately. The first term in
\eqref{eq:fast-bias-term-1} is the exponentially decaying contribution of the
initial condition. The second term involves the Taylor remainder. Since
$R_f$ is a second order remainder, there exists a constant $L_R>0$ such that:
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
    &\leq
    L_R
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        \bigl(\left \| \tilde x_{j} \right\|+\left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|\bigr)^2
     \right ]
    \\
    &\leq
    2L_R
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        \left \| \tilde x_{j} \right\|^2+\left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|^2
     \right ]
    \\
    &=
    2L_R
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        \left \| x_j-h(y_j)+h(y_j)-h(y^*) \right\|^2
        +
        \left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|^2
     \right ]
    \\
    &\leq
    2L_R
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        2\left \| x_j-h(y_j) \right\|^2
        +
        2\left \| h(y_j)-h(y^*) \right\|^2
        +
        \left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|^2
     \right ]
    \\
    &\leq
    2L_R
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        2\left \| x_j-h(y_j) \right\|^2
        +
        (2L_h^2+1)\left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|^2
     \right ] .
\end{align*}
Setting:
\[
    d_1 \triangleq \max\{2,\,2L_h^2+1\},
\]
we obtain:
\[
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
    \leq
    2L_Rd_1
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        \left \| x_j-h(y_j) \right\|^2
        +
        \left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|^2
     \right ] .
\]
Plugging this into the second term of~\eqref{eq:fast-bias-term-1} yields:
\begin{align*}
    \alpha\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)}
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    e^{-c\alpha(k-j)}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
    \leq
    2\alpha\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} L_R d_1
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    e^{-c\alpha(k-j)}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ 
        \left \| x_j-h(y_j) \right\|^2
        +
        \left \| \hat y_{j} \right\|^2
     \right ] .
\end{align*}

          
                                                                                                                                                           
               
               
                                                                                                                                                                                               
                                                                                                                                      
                                                                                                                                                                                                  
             
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     
                
                                                                                                            
                                                              
                                                                              
                                                                                       
                                                                      
              
                                                                                                                        
                
                                                                                                                                                                                                
              


By Lemma~\ref{lemma : Exp Sum Of the MSEs}, and using the notation
\[
    \mathcal M_{\alpha,\beta}
    \triangleq
    \mathrm{MSE}_{x}^{\infty}(\alpha,\beta)
    +
    \mathrm{MSE}_{y}^{\infty}(\alpha,\beta),
\]
we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \alpha \sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)}
    \sum_{j=0}^k
    e^{-c\alpha(k-j)}
    \mathbb{E} \left [ \left \| R_f(\tilde x_{j},\hat y_{j}) \right\| \right ]
    \leq\;&
    \alpha \underbrace{2\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} L_R d_1}_{\triangleq C_{x,1}}
    \left(
        \mathcal M_{\alpha,\beta}
        +
        u_k^{c,\alpha}
        +
        w_k^{c,\alpha}
    \right)
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    +
    \underbrace{2\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} L_R d_1 c^{-1}}_{ \triangleq C_{x,2}}
    \mathcal M_{\alpha,\beta}.
\end{align*}
The term involving $R_g$ is controlled in the same way. We denote $M_U \triangleq R_{ric} + \left \| U_0 \right\|_{\text{op}}$. Thus,
\begin{align}
    \eqref{eq:fast-bias-term-2}
    \leq\;&
    \beta \underbrace{2\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} M_U L_R d_1}_{\triangleq C_{x,3}}
    \left(
        \mathcal M_{\alpha,\beta}
        +
        u_k^{c,\alpha}
        +
        w_k^{c,\alpha}
    \right)
    \nonumber +\frac{\beta}{\alpha}
    \underbrace{2\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)} M_U L_R d_1 c^{-1}}_{C_{x,4}}
    \mathcal M_{\alpha,\beta}.
\end{align}
It remains to control the two weighted Markovian-noise terms in
\eqref{eq:fast-bias-term-3}. By Lemma~\ref{lemma:bias-poisson},
\begin{align*}
    \eqref{eq:fast-bias-term-3}
    \leq\;&
    \alpha A_1
    +
    \beta(A_2+B_1)
    +
    \alpha^2 A_3
    +
    \beta^2 B_2
    +
    \alpha\beta(A_4+B_3)
    +
    \frac{\beta^2}{\alpha}B_4 .
\end{align*}
where the constants are defined in detail in the proof of the lemma. Merging all the bounds from above we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \left \| s^x_{k+1} \right\|
    \leq\;&
    e^{-(k+1)c\alpha}\sqrt{\kappa(\mathrm L)}\left \| s^x_0 \right\|
    +
    (\alpha + \beta )F_x^{(1)}
    +
    F_x^{(2)}\mathcal M_{\alpha,\beta}
    +
    (\alpha +\beta ) F_x^{(3)}  (u_k^{c,\alpha}+w_k^{c,\alpha}) \nonumber \\
    & + R^x_{\alpha, \beta} 
\end{align*}
where the quantities $F_x^{(1)},F_x^{(2)},F_x^{(3)}, R^x_{\alpha, \beta}$ are defined by:
\begin{align*}
    F_x^{(1)}&\coloneqq A_1 \vee  (A_2 + B_1) \vee \omega^* B_4, \\
    F_x^{(2)} &\coloneqq C_{x,2}+ \omega ^* C_{x,4} + F_x^{(3)}(\alpha^* +\beta^*) \;,\\
    F_x^{(3)}&\coloneqq C_{x,3} \vee C_{x,1} \;,  \\
    R^x_{\alpha, \beta}&\coloneqq \alpha^2 A_3
    +
    \beta^2 B_2
    +
    \alpha\beta(A_4+B_3)
    \;.
\end{align*}
